102. EDWARD SMITH (25) , Feloniously forging and uttering a receipt for 5 l. with intent to defraud.
MR. MONTAGUE CHAMBERS with MR. HURRELL conducted the Prosecution; and MR. STRAIGHT the Defence.
JOHN REED . I am an engine-driver—in November, 1875, I was living with my father at 5, Ashdown, Buckstead, Sussex—at that time I opened an account at the Savings' bank at Uckfield; and I received this deposit-book; this is my name at the beginning; it was issued to me by the Uckfield post-master—on 27th November I deposited 11 l. there; my father went with me——on 15th January I deposited 2 l., and subsequently 7 l. 10 s. through my sister at Rotherfield post-office—since I paid in those sums I have never drawn any out, or authorised any one to do so for me—about 6th June I went to work at Midhurst—I was then lodging at my Bister's house once a month—she is the prisoner's wife; they lived in Town Row, Rotherfield—on a Monday evening in June I remember looking for my deposit-book and it was there all right; I kept it in a little box in my clothes box in the bedroom where I slept—I went away on 31st July and returned to my sister's on 26th August, and next day, Sunday, I examined my box and found that the little box was gone containing my deposit-book—I spoke to my sister about it after breakfast; the prisoner was present, and he said he would see if they had lost anything—he and my sister went upstairs and they said they had lost a ring and a. pair of black cloth trousers—I went to the post-office at Rotherfield that morning, my sister went with me, and I gave information to the post-master of my loss—I caused a letter to be written to the authorities, the prisoner wrote it at his own house; this is it ( produced)—I signed my name to it at the bottom. ( Bead: "August 27th, 1876. Dear sir,—I have had the misfortune to lose my bank-book from my box. I have paid 20 l. 10 s. altogether; 11 l. at Uckfield last November, when I began to pay; 2 l. at Uckfield, and 7 l. 10 s. at Rotherfield on 6th June. If no one has not taken the money out, I wish you would stop them, and let me know by return of post. I believe the number of my book is 1207. I had the book from Uckfield postoffice for 5, Ashdown. I live at Rotherfield now with my sister and brother. I have lost my letters that I had from you altogether with my book. Signed, John Reed, engine driver.") When I first deposited my money I signed this declaration—I did not sign this notice of withdrawal, nor is this my signature to this receipt on this warrant.
Cross-examined. Ikept the key of the little box in which my book was—my clothes box was not locked—when I ascertained my loss the prisoner suggested that I should at once write to the post-office, he said that was the. best thing I could do, and he sat down and wrote this letter; that was at his own house in Town Road, Rotherfield——Rotherfield is a very small place, there are a few scattered houses about there—the prisoner had the whole of the house—my sister is in service now; at that time she was hopping two
days before I came home in August, in Sussex, I believe—the prisoner works at hay tying; he did not do much work I think—he has one child about three or four months old—I think they have given up the house now——in August when my sister was hopping she came home at nights—the house was empty during the day.
Re-examined. The key of the large box was left in it, in the room where I slept——I asked my sister to take care of the key for me—the key was still in it when I came back and missed the little box—the large box stood at the foot of my bed——nothing else was taken out of the large box—the clothes I have on now were in it; my Sunday clothes, they did not take any of them; I had two or three shirts in there—nothing was moved but the little box.
GEORGE EDWARDS . I am post-master of the post-office at Rotherfield—on 2nd August I recollect a person coming and asking for a withdrawal notice; I can't tell at what time he came; it was some time during the day——the prisoner is the man that came——I asked him if he had got a savings' bank book, and he produced a deposit book; I can't say that this is the exact book; he produced a book, and I got him to fill up the notice from the book—it is the same number on the book, it is No. 1207, Uckfield—I handed the prisoner a withdrawal form; this is it; I told him how to fill it up; he filled it np and signed it in my presence. ( Read: "Uckfield, 1207, 5 l., Rotherfield, John Reed, Rotherfield, labourer.") He signed that "John Reed," and put in the word "labourer "—I then gave him the withdrawal notice and he posted it to the chief office—on the morning of 4th August I saw him again; he came into my office and wanted to know if there was a letter for John Reed; I told him the mail bag was not in yet; he said he would call again presently—in the course of about a quarter or half an-hour the mail bag came in; the letters were sorted out, and there was no letter for John Reed——in about half or three quarters of an hour the prisoner came again and produced this warrant; he did not say how he had got it, he simply produced it——I saw that it corresponded with the advice I had received, and I paid him the 5 l., and he signed the name "John Reed "on the warrant——that is a receipt—at the time I paid him the 5 l. I made this entry in the deposit book: "August 5th, 1876, K 65 l., 5 l., G.E."—I did not know the prisoner before by name, only by sight—as far as I knew he was John Reed.
Cross-examined. I saw him about after the 4th of August, and I saw him when the sergeant of police brought him to my office about three weeks after——the post-office is about a mile from where he resides—when the sergeant brought him it was about the 5 l. being withdrawn—I did at first say I would not swear to his being the man; it was in the evening—I swore to him before the Magistrate on the 4th September; I think that was three or four days after the sergeant had brought him to me——I had seen the prisoner in the interim and I had seen Mogford, the letter carrier; he comes to my office every morning—I had also seen the sergeant——the prisoner wrote the first document in my presence; he wrote it as anyone would, freely enough.
Re-examined. I recollect the prosecutor coming to me with his sister, the prisoner's wife, one Sunday morning——they'did not then bring me this letter, written by the prisoner, and signed by the prosecutor——they posted it in the afternoon——they did not show it me—I told them they had better send it, and they posted it—before they came to me 1 had no notion there
was anything wrong in the withdrawal of the 5 l.——I had nothing to do at my office with the withdrawal of the rest of the money.
EBENEZBR MOGFORD . I am employed as a letter carrier at Town Row, Rotherfield——on the 4th August I saw the prisoner there——he overtook me as I was going down to Town Row, and asked me if I had a letter for John Reed, if I had he would take it——I looked, and saw that I had a letter for John Reed, and I gave it to him——it was a similar letter to this, stamped with red ink, which means officially paid——I got that letter from Charles Payne, a letter carrier from Tunbridge Wells.
Crossexamined. I have delivered letters in Rotherfield for nearly two years——I have delivered to the prisoner several letters of his own, into his own hand—the people in Rotherfield sometimes meet me in the' street and ask me for their letters——this was on the 4th August——the prisoner had been to me two or three times before to wait for a letter——I don't know how long after the 4th of August it was that—I was spoken to about this; it was nearly a month before I went before the Magistrate.
Re-examined. I had never delivered any letters of Reed's to the prisoner before the 4th of August—I knew that his name was Edward Smith——I had known him about five months——his house was in my beat——I knew where he lived——it was Sergeant Avery who first asked me about this, and I then remembered that I had delivered such a letter to the prisoner for Reed.
EDWARD COOK EDE . I am in the principal Post-office Savings Bank, in London——when all the money is withdrawn the savings' bank-book is sent to oar office in London, and these warants-and receipts are issued from our office——I received this notice of withdrawal, and the warrant was sent back duly signed.
AMOS AVERT . I am a sergeant in the Sussex Constabulary——in consequence of information I received from my wife, on Sunday, 27th August, I went to Town Row, Rotherfield, to the prisoner's house—I saw him——I asked him what had been lost——he said "John has lost his box and bank-book, and I have lost a pair of trousers, a necktie, and a ring"——I searched round the premises to see if anybody had broken into the house——I found no trace of anything of the kind, nor anything like it——I searched the hedges and ditches round, and different places, supposing the box might have been thrown away——he said John had lost his box and his bank-book, and that his wife and John had gone to the post-office, and they could not find that any maney had been drawn—they resided alone in that house——on 31st August I obtained a warrant for the prisoner——I went and read the warrant over to him—he said "I know nothing at all about it"——I then locked him up—before that, on the 29th August, finding out that he had paid some money away at four or five places, I said to him "You have paid away some money, you have paid 1 l. to Isaac Diplock, of 5, Ashdown; 1 l. to Walter Noakes, 2 l. to your brother-in-law John; and you changed a sovereign at Bassett's beershop and a sovereign at Buckstead "—he said he had borrowed 2 l. Of Ned Bassett—I went to Bassett, and found he had not—I did not tell the prisoner what had passed between me and Bassett.
Cross-examined. I was examined before the Magistrate—I did not mention then anything about my conversation with the prisoner about these sums—I did not think it necessary to have it taken down—I did not give it—I did name it, but they did not think it necessaryto take it down—I did not give it in evidence—I left it to the clerk—I stated it, but it was not taken down—I am not stationed at Rotherfield—a man named
Gowers is there—Town Row is about half-way between me and him—the prisoner lives in a smallish house.
GUILTY . He also
PLEADED GUILTY to a previous conviction in July, 1872— Seven Years' Penal Servitude .

103. GEORGE WEBB (42), PLEADED GUILTY to a burglary in the dwelling house of Mary Henrietta Shelton, with intent to steal therein— Six Months' Imprisonment .

104. GEORGE PEARSON (38), was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Charles. Upsdell; he was also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like offence.
MR. BESLEY and MR. HORACE AVOEY conducted the Prosecution; and MR. WILLOUGHBY the Defence.
ALICE SPARSHOTT . I am the wife of Frederick Sparshott, and live at Hunston, in Sussex, next door to the deceased Charles Upsdell, his garden adjoining ours—it is separated by a high wall of Mr. Pearson's, and by a quick set hedge of Mr. Upsdell's, you can see through the hedge, I could not see into Pearson's garden—on Thursday afternoon, 26th October, I was in my garden and heard Pearson in Upsdell's garden—their gardens adjoined they run at right angles to mine, Upsdell's garden is on the right and Pearson's on the left—there is a high wall-between them—I heard both Pearson's and Upsdell's voices; I heard Pearson telling Mr. Upsdell "Your cows are in my garden "—Upsdell used bad language and said "I will do for you, and I will see it out Pearson "—I heard no more words, I heard blows, I could not see them, the wall separated us—the blows were in Pearson's garden—I saw them in Upsdell's garden and go towards Pearson's, and it was after that I heard the blows; I could not tell how many blows; there were more than two; they were heavy blows—I heard no voices at the time I heard the blows—I heard no more expressions after they left Upsdell's garden—the next thing I saw was Mr. Upsdell return to his own house by the hedge, the way he went; that was about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes after—he appeared to walk very weak, and he was covered with blood—I did not notice his head, he had no hat or cap—he was not doing anything with his hands, only walking to his own door.
Cross-examined. I had known both the men for a considerable time—the deceased was a man of very violent temper at times—I could not say whether or not he was sober that day, he was in the habit of getting drunk at times, and he was then disagreeable and violent—he was a big stout man, a powerful man, he always walked in a stooping position, with his head a good deal forward—he was older than the prisoner, the prisoner is somewhat exciteable at times—some little time ago he was pitched out of a carriage on his head; he recovered damages for that accident. he was on one occasion struck on the side of the head with a hammer by a Mr. Duke—I did not see it—I saw him return afterwards—I did not see him with his head bound up, I was not near him—I know that he was confined to his house in consequence of that injury, I saw him led home by a man—on this occasion, I was in my garden, rather in the back part of it; the deceased's language was foul and offensive, he spoke very loud and in a threatning manner—all I heard Paerson say was "Your cows are in my garden "—I did not hear him use threating or offensive language to the deceased—the blows I heard were
as if Upsdell was beating the calf; it sounded more like whacks, on an animal than on a human being—I had heard Upsdell beat his calf before, and the blows appeared to me similar to those.
Re-examined. Upsdell usually walked with his bent down, that was from age—he was nearly 65.
By THE COURT. Neither of them has anything in their bands when I saw them go towards Pearson's door.
JOSEPH HAMMOND . I am a labourer ar Hunstion, I lodged with Mr. Upsdell at a beer—house—on Thursday, 26th October, I was working in Mr. barchell's garden on the right of Person's—you cannot get from Upsdell's to Person's without going round the road of breaking through the hedge—Mr. Burehells garden where, I was working, is about 15 years from Pearson's garden—I heard talking between Pearson and Upsdell in Pearsons gardens; I coald not hear what they said for several mintites, it was very loud talking—I heard Upsdell say "You have done for me now"—them were all the words I heard—the voice seemed to come from the upper and of Pearsons garden, the end nearer Upsdell's garden than Burehell's—I went on with my work, and about ten minutes after I saw him. Upsdel—in consequence of what she said to me I went round to Upsdell gares and found him at the upper and of his own gardens towards the straw yard—he said "Help me get my cald in, see what Pearson has done for me"—he had his head tired up with a handkerechief, and he was covered with blood; blood was running from him then—I saw him walk indoors—soon after that I went for Dr. buckls—I did not see anything of person.
Cross-exmined. They used to have words sometimes and make it up again in about five minutes—I was about 15 yards from Pearson's garden when I heard the words, and Personal's garden in about 35 yards long, I was about 45 yards or so front where, I heard the voice, and it did not strike me as anything the matter.
By THE COURT. I thought it was a joke—I did not konw what to think when I saw him bleeding, it frightemed me.
ELLEN UPSDELL . I am the widow of the deceasd—on 26th October, I saw him and pearson go away—about half—way down my garden, Pearson turned round and said. "Your calf is in my garden, my husband followed and passed him about half—way down the garden, I don't know what passed between them afterwards, my husband came back in about fifteen or twenty minutes bleeding very much from the head and marking a dreadful noise, groaning very much—he knew what he was about—I ask him down in the chair and washed his head—he said "Go for Hammond and get the calf out of the garden," I did so, and Hammond got the calf cut of the garden—when I came back my husband was in the garden with a towel on the top of his head, I asked him to come in; I got him in and laid him on the sofa, and he bled very much indeed and screamed, and said Pearson had hit him—I could scarcely hold nim, he seemed racked with pain—I asked Hammond to go in for Mr. buckled immediately, and Mr. buckle came within about half an hour afterwards—when my husband went out that afternoon he half a cap on his head, which I afterwards gave to the doctor—he had it on when he came in again—this is it ( produced)—when he went but it was a very good cap, there was not a hole in it, when he came in there was this cut in it—when I got him on the sofa. he took it off his head and put it on the table, and then the doctor came—the peak of the cao was good when he went cut, when he came back it was torn like this—he died on the wednesday following, 1st November.
Cross-examined. My husband and the prisoner had a good many dealings together at times—I believe on this day Pearson wanted him to sell him some straw, and my husband said he had no more than than he should want during the winter for his own cattle—they sometimes had a drop—I don't know whether they had had drop together that day—Pearson was in our house very little that day, he was in and out a good deal most days—our garden and his join by a quick set hedge, and he used to go down our garden and make a backway through our hedge—occasionally they used to have words, but they made it up—the dispute was a good deal because he would come in the backway, he cut his fence down, and my husband was very particular about the cattle going into his premises, he put up posts and rails, and then Pearson made a way through—when my husband came in I said "Lord bless me, what is the matter % * and he said that Pearson had hit him with a pole; that was the second time he came in—after I had washed his head and sent for Hammond, he went out again and was driving the calf, and said to him "Come in," and it was then he said that Pearson had hit him with a pole—he was quite conscious up to the last—the house was quite quiet during his illuess—the room he was in was over the taproom, and we had customers coming in and out all the evening; I kept him as quiet as I could.
WILLIAM BENNETT BARTON FREELAND . I am a member of the firm of Draper & Freeland, clerks to the County Justices at Chichester—on 27th October, I was present with the superintendent of police at Upsdell's house—the prisoner was taken there in custody; I heard the deceased in his presence make a statement which I reduced into writing—the prisoner cross-examined him—Upsdell made his mark and the Justice signed it. ( Read: "The deposition of Charles Upsdell, taken in the presence of George Pearson. I live here, I have lived twenty years in this house; the prisoner George Pearson lives a few doors from me; I was in Pearson's garden about 4 o'clock this evening; I went after my calf which had got into Pearson's garden. He knocked me down with-a great stick about four times, he hit me. The stick was about 10 feet long, it was a clothes prop. He knocked my head all to pieces, I was 10 feet from him. He did not say a word to me, nor I to him. I fell on the ground from the first blow. He hit me three times afterwards. After the first blow was struck, while I was on the ground he said I was not a going to take the calf out of his garden. I got up again and drove the calf off the premises. Another man, my lodger, Joseph Hammond, came to my assistance, I was covered with blood—when I went into the garden, Pearson came from the granary with the pole, in about an hour or more. We had some conversation about some straw; he wanted a barrow load of my straw for his pig. He wheeled it out of the barn in my wheelbarrow. We had no words about the straw, I sold it to him for 3 d. I have never had any quarrel with him. After he had struck me he put the prop behind the granary. He did not come to my assistance, Joseph Hammond came to me. I called out for Joe to help me take the calf out of the garden. I was able to walk into the house afterwards; I sent for the doctor soon after I came in.
Cross-examined by the Prisoner. You brought me in 4 lbs. of butter, about two hours or more before you struck me; I did not see you after I got into the garden before you struck me. I did not say when you drove the calf out 'Don't disturb the b——you did not leave me and go indoors till after you had struck me; you had a little straw in my barrow for your pig in the sty."
RICHARD MAYNARD ( Constable West Sussex Constabulary.); On 26th October, I went to the deceased's house, and afterwards, in consequence of what Dr. Buckle said to me, I went with him to the prisoner's house—another constable went to the back, I went to the front; after ringing two or three times, the prisoner looked out from the upstairs window—I told him that Dr. Buckle and myself wanted to speak to him—he came down, and we went into his living room—I told him I was going to take him into custody on the moat serious charge of assaulting Mr. Upsdell, at the same time I cautioned him that whatever he said I should give in evidence against him—he replied "The last time I saw Upsdell was in my garden"—I then asked him to go through his back door into his garden—we went out and made a search, but found nothing—I took the prisoner to Chichester, and afterwards, by the superintendent's orders, I took him back to Hunston, and there Upsdell's depositions were taken in his presence—next morning, about 5 am., I went into the prisoner's garden, it is divided into three cross-paths, and in the third bed I found a large pool of blood; it was about twelve paces from the back of the granary, and about 10 or yards from the back door of Pearson's house—I traced blood from the large pool along the garden path through the hedge into Upsdell's meadow, and almost close up to his back door—I made another search, and found this pole ( produced) hanging up by the granary in Pearson's garden, exactly where Upsdell said he had taken it from—I continued my search, and found this chopper ( produced), about twenty-two strides from the pool of blood; it was chucked down about two yards from a chopping block—there was something sticking to the back which induced me to carry it to the doctor; it was some flick, or something, which I considered came from the deceased's cap.
Cross-examined. This bill-hook has got no handle—I did not tell the prisoner of Upsdell's death; he knew it—when I took him into custody, I charged him with the assault—he remained in custody for the assault after Upsdell's death—he said it was a bad job—he did not say he was sorry for it—the superintendent is not here; the prisoner did not say that the deceased had assaulted him, not to me or to anyone else—I was present the whole of the time; when the superintendent told himthat Upsdell was dead, he said "It is a bad job; what will they do with me now?"—that was all he said—he did not say that Upsdell had attacked him.
Re-examined. He was in the cell at the police-station when the superintendent told him that Upsdell was dead; I was by the side of the superintendent at the time—I remained there on duty for hours; I was there all the time the superintendent was there and for hours afterwards, and I am quite sure that neither to me or the superintendent did he say that he had been assaulted.
ERNEST COOK BUCKLE . I am a duly qualified surgeon, practising at Chichester—I was called on to attend the deceased about 5 o'clock on Thursday afternoon, 25th October, for a wound in the head—he had lost blood—the third finger of his left hand was bruised; it appeared to be a recent bruise, such as you get in a jamb—on the left wrist there was another bruise and slight abrasions; and on the forehead and nose there were slight scratches—upon a post-mortem examination I found a clean incised wound, about 4 inches long, on the left parietal bone, going right through the scalp, both plates of the skull, into the brain itself—there were no signs of bruising about the wound—there was no other wound on the head—on throwing back the scalp so as to lay bare the skull, there was no sign of bruising—the cut in the skull was a clean cut, the edges were
sharply defined, and it terminated more abruptly at the anterior part, that is towards the forehead, than the posterior; I mean that it was more perpendicular, not so sloping, as if made with an instrument thicker at the bottom than the top—there was a slight depressed fracture at the anterior part as well; that is what is called a star fracture; it was about the size of a fourpenny piece; the inner plate of the skull was driven down between 1/8 and 1/4 of an inch; the greater part of 'the wound was 1/4 of an inch deep, but in the centre of the wound, in the brain, it went very nearly £ of an inch—the width of the fracture of the bone was about 1/8 of an inch—the width of the external wound on the scalp was retracted to 1/2 an inch or more—when you cut the tissues they alway come back directly from muscular contraction—there was no indication whatever of a blunt instrument on the scalp itself—it was a clean cut wound—in my opinion it was impossible that it could have been done by this pole—I compared the billhook with the wound, and in my opinion such an instrument would produce such a wound—the deceased's cap was shewn to me by Mrs. Upsdell—I find in it now a perforation corresponding with the wound on the head; there is a cut about 4 inches long on the left side of the cap, it goes through the outer part and after that also goes through a small layer of wadding and through the lining of the cap—the blow must have been a violent one, with a sharp cutting instrument, to go through the cap, the lining, and down to the substance of the brain—you often get the star fracture by the actual violence without anything actually touching it—it is a kind of a spreading out of the force—the primary cause of death was inflammation, produced by the wound; he died from the effects of the violence to which he had been subjected—from the character of the wound I should think he must have been stooping at the time the blow was struck, or he might possibly have been on the ground; I can't say—he was a taller man than the prisoner—I should say the person who struck the blow was in front of him—the bruises I have spoken of might have been caused at the same time, by the back of the same weapon, I mean the graze on the wrist and the jamb on the finger.
Cross-examined. I made a microscopic examination of what was on the bill-hook, not of the bill-hook itself—I could find no recent marks of its having been knocked about—it is very blunt—I examined it on the Saturday, the 28th, following the Thursday—I stated before the Magistrate "There were two hairs adhering to it which were human hairs, but they had evidently been there some time "—that had reference to the pole—there were either rabbit or hare hairs, on the bill-hook, they corresponded with either—I was before the Magistrate and also before the coroner—the deceased never complained of being struck with a sharp instrument—I have been qualified as a medical man two years—there was a scalp wound.
Q. Is this correct: "It not unfrequently happens that bruised wounds of the scalp present the appearance of a clean cut, and look exactly as if they had been produced by some sharp instrument?"— A. They might do so, but I have never seen one; all the cut wounds I have seen in the hospital have been bruised as well; I have never seen a single one without—I do not know Mr. Prescott Hewitt personally—I would not undertake to differ with him if he says "Star wounds are for the most part accompanied by bruising of the soft part, but it not unfrequently happens that they present the appearance of a clean cut, as if produced by a sharp instrument"—the parietal bone is about the same consistency as most bones; it is not a very
thick bone—this cut penetrated through the inner and outer table of the skull.
MR. WILLOUGHBY, in reply to the Court, stated that he could not resist a verdict of Manslaughter, the act of the prisoner being an unlawful one; the only question being with what instrument the 'wound was inflicted.
GUILTY — Fifteen Months' Imprisonment
Before Mr. Baron Hawkins. '
[trialAccount t18761120-105]
105. SARAH TULETT (24) , Feloniously causing grievous bodily harm to her new-born child with intent to murder it.
MR. RAVEN conducted the Prosecution; and MR. BOXALL defended the Prisoner.
WILLIAM PABKER ( Brighton Police). On Sunday morning, the 9th July, I was on duty in the London Road, Preston, about 7.30 a.m., and saw the prisoner walking in the direction of Brighton, from Preston—she had a bundle on her left arm—I wished her good morning, and she said "I wish you good morning, sir; what time does the train leave for Burgess Hill, as I have left my situation, and am going there for a few days; I have another one to go into, and am going to see some friends "—I was then close to her—I told her that I could not tell her what time the train started—I heard a noise inside her bundle, and asked her what the bundle contained—she said "Dirty clothes "—I told her that I must look inside the bundle, and see what there was, as there was something besides dirty clothes—she said nothing until I began to open the top part of the bundle, when she said "Oh! do let me go and bury it, for it is quite dead "—I saw the head of a child, and told her that it was not dead, for I could distinctly hear it making a noise, and that I must take her to the station, and I did so—the parcel was opened there, and it contained a female child, with the string of a petticoat tied three times round its neck, and the ends tucked underneath the folds—I sent for a cab, and took the prisoner to the work-house infirmary—the string was very tight.
COURT. Q. It could not have been on very tight or the child would have died, would it not, before you got to the station? A. It was wound round three times; it was not so very tight—the child's breathing resembled that of a puppy; it laboured very much when it was drawing its breath, I could see that—it appeared to be a great relief to the child when the string was taken off, it breathed much easier, and then began to cry—I asked the prisoner on the way to the station, where the child was born, she said in a field near the church at Preston, at 4 o'clock in the morning.
Cross-examined. What first attracted, my notice to the prisoner was that I saw her looking very ill, and a man told me she had been sitting by the church gate for three quarters of an hour—she is very deaf; she did not seem excited when she spoke to me—the noise inside the parcel attracted my attention—it was a parcel done up in brown paper, carried underneath her waterproof—the noise I heard was a cry resembling that of a puppy just born, or more of a moaning—I could not have heard it if I had been so far off as that door—I was touching her—she did not appear to hear the noise at all—I was about a mile from the police-station when I took her into custody, and she walked that distance with me—she carried the parcel, but I assisted her—I took hold of her arm, and assisted her along; she was in a very weak condition—she was not fainting then, but she did after I got to the station, and I got a cab—it took me about twenty-five minntes to get her to the station, and during that time I had not opened the bundle except
merely at the top, so that for twenty-five minutes the string which was, as I say, tight round the neck, had not been removed, and yet the child was breathing—I had only opened the parcel just enough to see the child's head—I did not see its face then because I saw she was in a very weak state, and I wanted to get her to the station—I did not think at the time that the child was in state to require immediate assistance—I could not judge whether the cry was that of a child undergoing the process of strangulation; at any rate, I did not open it till I got to the station—I was there when it was opened; the parcel was about 2 feet long, with several dirty petticoats, and the child inside them, and it was done up in brown paper—a petticoat was round the child—the string was still attached to the petticoat and the petticoat was wrapped round the child, and the brown paper round the petticoat—the petticoat was retained in its place by means of the tape round the neck—the end of the string, which was attached to the petticoat was the first round the child; I mean that the part of the string which was fastened to the petticoat; the part nearest to the petticoat went round the neck first—I went to the workhouse with the prisoner in a cab, and took the child with me—no medical man saw the child till some time after we got to the workhouse—two minutes after we got to the station I assisted in taking the string off the child's neck—we simply unwound it, we did not cut it, we simply took hold of the end and pulled it out—it came out. easily.
Re-examined. I took the prisener into custody when I saw the head of the child—the noise of the child first attracted my attention; it sounded like a child that had something round its neck—I cannot say whether the noise was like that of a child being suffocated—it was a free sound.
ELIZA COOK . I am a widow, and live at 1, Robert Street, Brighton—on 8th July, the prisoner hired my room for two nights, and came into it about 10 o'clock that evening—I heard her about very early in the morning,. which caused me to get up, and I saw her go out about 6 o'clock and cross the road, and turn up North Road; that is in the direction of Preston—she turned the corner, and I did not see her again till I saw her at the Town Hall—at 9 o'clock, a policeman came and told me something, in consequence of which I went into the room and examined the bed, and was fully convinced that a birth had taken place there, as I saw what is usual on such occasions—I am able to say that from my own knowledge.
Cross-examined. There was no appearance of concealment in the room more than decency required—I did not notice that the prisoner brought anything with her when she came.
DAVID RICHARDS . I am a surgeon to the work house infirmany, Brighton—on the morning of 9th July, the prisoner was admitted to the lying in ward, at about 8.30—I examined her and found that she had been recently delivered, but it was not complete; the child had been born, but the after birth had not been taken away—she said nothing at that time—I examined the child and found that the string had been cut, about a foot from the body and there was a mark round the neck, evidently produced by some string or tape; it was a narrow mark—the tape is not here; it was cut off—the neck was reddened as if from the pressure of a piece of string or tape; the skin was broken, and it took about a fortnight to heal—as far as I could judge that wound was caused by the pressure of a string.
By THE COURT. It was an abrasion of the skin and it ulcerated afterwards—the mark was just over the trachea—that is a spot where if there is
any tight pressure, even on a grown person, it would be serious, a grown person would be rendered absolutely powerless in a moment by any strong pressure there—the mark might have been accidental—a ligature round the neck must have been tight to produce that mark—supposing that the string was so tight as to cause the abrasion, it would be impossible for a newly born child to bear it.
By MR. RAVEN. In my opinion the string might have been tighter and become slackened—I believe that was accidental—the prisoner afterwards told me in the course of the day that she did not know what possessed her to try and kill the child—the child stayed in the infirmary, and the prisoner left well, on August 8th. ( The child was now in the prisoner's arms.)
Cross-examined. She was in bed when I first saw her—I had to use ordinary force to complete the labour; it was not the process of nature—I merely asked her how it was she got into this trouble, and she told me she did not know what possessed her to try and kill the child—I think she heard my question—when I said "this trouble," I meant being arrested—she was in a very weak state, but she was able to speak; she had rallied then; she was walking about the ward in nine days or a fortnight, and the child took about a fortnight to recover; it was with its mother all the time, and I treated it for a laceration of the neck—the wound was about 1/8 of an inch long all round the neck—it was an abrasion—in newly-born children very little is required to make a serious wound—there is not a tendency in the, folds of the neck to show very bad signs, but there is lower down—I do not think the same cause that would bring forward a tendency to produce those marks would cause laceration—there was a mark on the windpipe—no lasting mark is left on the child—I was in Court and heard the whole of the case before the committing Magistrate—the prisoner is very deaf and there was no" one defending her—I did not hear the answers she made to the Magistrate's questions.
WILLIAM PARKER ( re-examined). I was present before the Magistrate and heard the statement the prisoner made—she said "If the—string got there it got there by accident," The deposition being refered to only stated that ike prisoner said "I have nothing to say.").
NOT GUILTY .
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, DECEMBER 11 TH, 1876.
